ORDER
Before the Court is a Joint Motion to Approve Consent Judgment and Permanent Injunction (Motion) (Doc. 19). Therein, the Parties represent they “have reached a settlement of all claims and counterclaims in this Litigation.” (Id. at 1). The Parties request entry of a consent judgment and permanent injunction against Plaintiff. (Id.). Parties also filed a “Joint Stipulation of Dismissal With Prejudice . . . with each Party to bear its own costs and fees.” (Id. at 2). Defendant subsequently filed a Motion to Enforce Settlement Agreement and Memorandum in Support (Doc. 21) and Reply (Doc. 22) requesting the Court approve and enforce the Proposed Consent Judgment and Permanent Injunction.
Defendant avers that it is unable to enforce the injunction on Amazon.com without the Court’s ruling on the Consent Judgment and Permanent Injunction. (Doc. 21 at 2).
PROCEDURAL BACKGROUND
On December 12, 2025, Plaintiffs filed a Complaint for Declaratory Judgment against Defendant for disputed claims of violations and infringement of U.S. Patent No.
12,390,016 (‘016 Patent), invalidity of ‘016 Patent and tortious interference with business relationships. (Doc. 1). On February 23, 2026, Defendant filed an Answer and Counterclaims to the Complaint averring ‘016 Patent and U.S. Patent No. 12,433,419 (‘419 Patent) are exclusively licensed to Defendant; and Plaintiffs’ advertisement and selling of Case 7:25-cv-00193-LAG Document 23 Filed 09/11/26 Page 1 of 11

2 items identified in Doc. 10 ¶ 35 infringe on Defendant’s ‘016 Patent and ‘419 Patent—a violation of Defendant’s exclusive licensee of the Patents. (See Doc. 10 ¶¶ 35, 74–93).
Plaintiffs filed their Counterclaim Answer on March 16, 2026. (Doc. 14). On March 27, 2026, the Parties filed a Joint Motion for Protective Order (Doc. 15) and subsequently, requested the Court stay all deadlines and proceedings for settlement negotiations. (Doc.
17). The Court granted the stay on May 4, 2026. (Doc. 18). On May 29, 2026, the instant Motion was filed. (Doc. 19).

In the Joint Motion to Approve Consent Judgment and Permanent Injunction, “Plaintiffs consent to the entry of the . . . Consent Judgment, which includes a finding of infringement of [the ‘016 Patent and ‘419 Patent], and a permanent injunction against Plaintiffs and their respective agents, officers, employees, and all persons acting in concert with them.” (Id. ¶ 2). After reviewing the filed Proposed Consent Judgment and Permanent Injunction, counsel emailed the Court to indicate the “terms” of the Proposed Consent Judgment and Permanent Injunction “was intended by the Parties to apply specifically to Plaintiffs in this case,” not other unrepresented third parties, and offered the following underlined correction to the Schedule 1 terminology:

The term “Accused Products” also includes any products made, used, sold, offered for sale, or imported by Plaintiffs that are substantially similar to or colorable imitations of the products identified above, including but not limited to products sold under different platforms, ASINs, brand names, or product names that embody the same or substantially similar bed sheet retention system design and functionality.

Email from Armon Shahdadi, Counsel for Defendant, to Marcia Alvarez Benavidez, Courtroom Deputy, U.S. District Court for the Middle District of Georgia (July 23, 2026, 3:47 PM) (on file with the Court); see also (Doc. 19-1 at 14). The Proposed Consent Judgment states,
“[t]he ’016 Patent and ’419 Patent are valid and enforceable[; and] Plaintiffs . . . acknowledge and agree that the bed sheet retention products identified in Schedule 1 to th[e] Consent Judgment, and substantially similar products and colorable imitations thereof (collectively, the ‘Accused Products’), Case 7:25-cv-00193-LAG Document 23 Filed 09/11/26 Page 2 of 11

3 infringe one or more claims of each of the ’016 Patent and ’419 Patent under 35 U.S.C. § 271, either literally or under the doctrine of equivalents”

(Doc. 19-1 ¶¶ 4–5).

The Proposed Consent Judgment and Permanent Injunction enjoins “Plaintiffs and their respective officers, agents, servants, employees, attorneys, affiliates, subsidiaries, successors, assigns, and all persons acting in active concert or participation with any of them, and all entities owned by or under common ownership with them,” from making, using, selling, or importing into the U.S. the Accused Products in Schedule 1. (Id. ¶¶ 8.a.– b.; see id. at 6–14). Plaintiffs are enjoined from “inducing or contributing to the infringement by others” of the Patents for which Defendant is the exclusive licensee, including enjoinment from “listing, advertising, marketing, or promoting on Amazon.com or any other online or offline marketplace or platform” and Plaintiffs shall not infringe or induce or contribute to infringement of the “copyright owned by or exclusively licensed to [Defendant] . . . .” (Id. ¶¶ 8.a.–e.). Plaintiffs are further enjoined from “engaging in email spoofing, impersonation, false or bad-faith reports to Amazon.com or any other marketplace . . . or other conduct interfering with [Defendant’s] enforcement of its intellectual property rights or th[e] Consent Judgment.” (Id. ¶ 8.f.).
Finally, the Proposed Consent Judgment and Permanent Injunction dismisses with prejudice all claims by Plaintiff and Defendant’s Counterclaim Counts III and IV, with each Party to bear its own costs and fees. (Id. ¶¶ 9–10). Pursuant to the Proposed Consent and Permanent Injunction, the Court retains jurisdiction over the Parties and the subject matter for enforcement purposes, with the Plaintiff waiving “any defense of lack of personal jurisdiction, improper venue, or forum non conveniens.” (Id. ¶¶ 11–12).
On June 29, 2026, Defendant filed a Motion to Enforce the Settlement Agreement and Memorandum in Support. (Doc. 21). In the Motion to Enforce the Settlement Agreement, Defendant avers that Amazon Marketplace continues to have active listings that are covered by the Proposed Consent Judgment and Permanent Injunction. (Doc. 21 at 2). Defendant states that “[e]ntry of the proposed Consent Judgment would provide” the Case 7:25-cv-00193-LAG Document 23 Filed 09/11/26 Page 3 of 11

4 required court order to get the listings taken down. (Id.). Defendant requests the Court enter the Proposed Consent Judgment and Permanent Injunction to protect its ability to enforce its intellectual property rights. (Id. at 1).

LEGAL STANDARD
“District courts should approve consent decrees so long as they are not unconstitutional, unlawful, unreasonable, or contrary to public policy.” Stovall v. City of Cocoa, Fla., 117 F.3d 1238, 1240 (11th Cir. 1997) (citations omitted). A consent decree, however, “does not merely validate a compromise but, by virtue of its injunctive provisions, reaches into the future and has [a] continuing effect, [thus] its terms require more careful scrutiny.” Id. at 1242 (quoting U.S. v. City of Miami, Fla., 664 F.2d 435, 441 (5th Cir. 1981)). The Court must verify the terms are fair and determine “the proposal represents a reasonable factual and legal determination based on the facts of record,” which entails a record that is proven by evidence in the complaint, affidavits, or factual stipulations. Id. (quoting City of Miami, Fla., 664 F.2d at 441). A court, however, must examine [a consent judgment] carefully to ascertain not only that it is a fair settlement but also that it does not put the court’s sanction on and power behind a decree that violates [the] Constitution, statute, or jurisprudence. . . . [And i]f the decree also affects third parties, the court must be satisfied that the effect on them is neither unreasonable nor proscribed.

Id. (quoting City of Miami, Fla., 664 F.2d at 441) (emphasis omitted).
In entering any injunction, the order must include a statement of the reasons why it issued, terms of specificity, and “in reasonable detail . . . the act or acts restrained or required.” Fed. R. Civ. P. 65(d)(1). The injunction can only bind “the parties[,] . . . the parties’ officers, agents, servants, employees, and attorneys[,] and” persons who are in active concert with a party and who receive actual notice. Fed. R. Civ. P. 65(d)(2); see also Trump v. CASA, Inc., 606 U.S. 831, 851 (2025) (“The equitable tradition has long embraced the rule that courts generally may administer complete relief between the parties.”
(emphasis in original) (internal quotation omitted)). Thus, a court must verify the proposed injunctive language complies with Rule 65(d)’s requirements even with a consent Case 7:25-cv-00193-LAG Document 23 Filed 09/11/26 Page 4 of 11

5 judgment. See Combs v. Ryan’s Coal Co., Inc., 785 F.2d 970, 978 (11th Cir. 1986) (“while the preference is to enforce the requirements of Rule 65(d) ‘scrupulously,’ . . . [t]he appropriate inquiry is into the degree of specificity of the language of the decree in order to determine that appellate review is possible and that the contemnors knew their obligations.”). It is also the prerogative of the Court to keep jurisdiction in a case with a consent judgment and permanent injunction. See Spallone v. U.S., 493 U.S. 265, 276 (1990) (“In selecting a means to enforce the consent judgment, the [d]istrict [c]ourt [i]s entitled to rely on the axiom that courts have inherent power to enforce compliance with their lawful orders through civil contempt.” (internal quotation omitted)).
DISCUSSION
I. Complaint, Counterclaims and Consent Judgment
In accordance with the proposed consent judgment, all of Plaintiffs’ claims (Counts I–III) and two of Defendant’s counterclaims (Counterclaim Counts III and IV) are dismissed with prejudice; and two of Defendant’s counterclaims (Counterclaim Count I and II) are validated. (See Doc. 19-1 ¶¶ 9–10). With regard to the validity of Defendant’s Counterclaim Counts I and II the Court considers whether there is adequate factual and legal support. See Stovall, 117 F.3d at 1142; (Docs. 1, 10, 14). Counterclaim Counts I and II allege infringement on Defendant’s exclusive licensure of ‘016 Patent and ‘419 Patent under 35 U.S.C. § 271. (Doc. 10 ¶¶ 74–79, 80–85). Pursuant to 35 U.S.C. § 217,
(a) . . . whoever, without authority makes, uses, offers to sell, or sells any patented invention, within the United States or imports into the United States any patented invention during the term of the patent therefor, infringes the patent.

(b) Whoever actively induces infringement of a patent shall be liable as an infringer.

(c) Whoever offers to sell or sells within the United States or imports into the United States a component of a patented machine, manufacture, combination or composition, or a material or apparatus for use in practicing a patented process, constituting a material part of the invention, knowing the same to be especially made or especially adapted for use in an infringement of such patent, and not a staple article or Case 7:25-cv-00193-LAG Document 23 Filed 09/11/26 Page 5 of 11

6 commodity of commerce suitable for substantial noninfringing use, shall be liable as a contributory infringer.

A. Findings of Infringement
Having reviewed the record, the Court finds as follows:

1. The Court has subject matter jurisdiction over this matter and personal jurisdiction over the Parties. (Doc. 1 ¶ 8; Doc. 10 ¶¶ 6–8; see Doc. 19-1 ¶ 1).
2. Defendant is the exclusive licensee of ‘016 Patent and ‘419 Patent, with the right to sue for infringement and collect damages. (Doc. 10-1 at 2– 48; Doc. 10-2 at 2–68; Doc. 10-3 at 2–3; see Doc. 19-1 ¶¶ 2–3); 35 U.S.C.
§ 271.

3. ‘016 Patent and ‘419 Patent are valid and enforceable. (Doc. 10-1 at 2–48; Doc. 10-2 at 2–68; see Doc. 19-1 ¶ 4).

4. Plaintiffs’ bed sheet retention products identified in Schedule 1 (Doc.
19-1 at 6–14) (collectively, the Accused Products), are substantially similar products and colorable imitations thereof, and infringe one or more claims of each of the ‘016 Patent and ‘419 Patent under 35 U.S.C. § 271, either literally or under the doctrine of equivalents.

1
B. Consent Judgment
Next, the Court reviews the terms of the Proposed Consent Judgment and Permanent Injunction to determine if it is “unconstitutional, unlawful, unreasonable, or contrary to public policy.” Stovall, 117 F.3d at 1240. Nothing in the proposal is unlawful or contrary to public policy or the Constitution; rather, enforcement of the proposed consent judgment vindicates the exclusive property rights of Defendant. The Court further finds the Parties’ terms reasonable considering the facts and circumstances surrounding the infringement and retaliatory actions by Plaintiffs when Defendant tried to enforce its rights as the exclusive licensee of ‘016 Patent and ‘419 Patent. (See Doc. 10 ¶¶ 50–73; Exhibit V, Doc. 10-22 at 2– 11; Exhibit W, Doc. 10-23 at 2; Exhibit X, Doc. 10-24 at 2–4; Exhibit Y, Doc. 10-25 at 2).
1 (See Doc. 19-1 ¶ 5).

Case 7:25-cv-00193-LAG Document 23 Filed 09/11/26 Page 6 of 11

7
II. Permanent Injunction
For the Court to order a permanent injunction as part of the consent judgment, the Proposed Consent Judgment and Permanent Injunction must contain, “the reasons why [the permanent injunction] issued; . . . state [the] terms specifically; and . . . describe in reasonable detail—and not by referring to the complaint or other document—the act or acts restrained or required.” Fed. R. Civ. P. 65(d)(1). The Court issues this permanent injunction to protect Defendant’s rights as the exclusive licensee of the ‘016 Patent and ‘419 Patent and provides clear terms and details of the acts from which Plaintiffs are enjoined.
(Doc. 19-1 ¶¶ 8.a.–f.). The permanent injunction is enforceable against “Plaintiffs and their respective officers, agents, servants, employees, attorneys, affiliates, subsidiaries, successors, assigns, and all persons acting in active concert or participation with any of them, and all entities owned by or under common ownership with them,” and sufficiently is limited pursuant to Fed. R. Civ. P. 65(d)(2).

CONCLUSION
Accordingly:

1. The Parties’ Joint Motion (Doc. 19) is GRANTED.

2. Any pending motions are DENIED as moot and the Clerk is DIRECTED to terminate all deadlines and close this case.

3. The Clerk is further DIRECTED to enter final judgment in favor of Defendant and against Plaintiffs.

4. The Court retains jurisdiction to enforce the Consent Judgment and Permanent Injunction.

5. The following Consent Judgment and Permanent Injunction is ENTERED with Schedule 1 attached to this Order.

Deaton v. Johnson Opinion and Order

IN THE UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT

OPINION AND ORDER
Before the Court are Defendants’ Motion to Reopen Case and for Injunctive Relief and Brief in Support (ECF Nos. 96–97); Plaintiffs’ Objection to the Motion to Reopen Case and for Injunctive Relief and Brief in Support (ECF Nos. 102–103); and Defendants’ Reply and Appendix in Support (ECF Nos. 107–108). Having considered the Motions, briefing, and applicable law, the Court DENIES Defendants’ Motion to Reopen the Case and for Injunctive Relief (ECF No. 96).
All other motions are DENIED as moot (ECF No. 100).

I. BACKGROUND1
This case arises from a dispute between John Deaton and Steven Johnson that took place over ten years ago. In 2008, Plaintiffs John Deaton, a Rhode Island attorney, and his law firm (“Plaintiff”) and Defendants Steven Johnson and his Law firm (the “JLF Defendants”) entered into an agreement in which Plaintiff would serve as local counsel for a variety of JLF Defendants’ clients in Rhode Island for cases related to personal injury suits regarding Kugel Mesh.
1 Unless otherwise cited, the Court’s recitation of the facts is taken from Defs.’ Mot. Reopen Case and Injunctive Relief, ECF No. 96–97.

Case 4:23-cv-00415-O Document 110 Filed 09/14/26 Page 1 of 12 PageID <pageID>

- 2 -
In 2016, a disagreement arose because Plaintiff claimed he was entitled to $1,000,000 in fees related to his representation of JLF Defendants’ clients in Rhode Island. Plaintiff first sued JLF Defendants in Rhode Island Superior Court in 2016. Justice Alice Gibney (Ret.), without adjudicating the underlying dispute between parties, ordered that $1,000,000 be segregated in an escrow account until all disputes were resolved (“Segregation Order”). Accordingly, Garretson Resolution Group (“GRG”), a third-party settlement fund administrator, segregated $1,000,000 of the settlements from the Kugel Mesh cases.

Around the same time in 2017, Plaintiff was involved in a lawsuit against JLF Defendants in the Tarrant County district court. Deaton v. Johnson, 2017 WL 2991939, at *3 (Tex. App. Ct.
July 14, 2017). In that case, Defendant Blake Norvell (“Norvell”). a Texas attorney, represented Margaret Moreno (“Moreno”) in a legal malpractice action against Plaintiff and the JLF Defendants. The action arose in connection with Plaintiff and the JLF Defendants’ failure to distribute settlement funds to Moreno from a class action in which both JLF Defendants and Plaintiff represented her. The 48th Judicial District in Tarrant County, Texas referred this malpractice action to Arbitration. Both Plaintiff and JLF Defendants were co-defendants in this action. During the arbitration, Plaintiff and JLF Defendants subsequently filed crossclaims against each other based on their underlying fee-sharing agreement which formed the basis of their joint representation of Moreno. The crossclaims included breach of contract and fraud pertaining to their fee-sharing agreement. Here, Plaintiff had the opportunity to raise any claims that he had against JLF Defendants related to his representation of JLF Defendants clients in the Kugel Mesh cases. So, he did.

Arbitration began in 2018 in Texas between Plaintiff and JLF Defendants and concluded on March 29, 2022, when the arbitrator entered a final arbitration award (“Final Arbitration Case 4:23-cv-00415-O Document 110 Filed 09/14/26 Page 2 of 12 PageID <pageID>

- 3 -
Award”).

2 Plaintiff was awarded $135,000 for his quantum meruit claim, while all his other claims were denied. In response, the Tarrant County District Court entered a final judgment on July 5, 2022, confirming the Final Arbitration Award.

After all of Plaintiff’s claims had been arbitrated and adjudicated by the Texas state courts, Plaintiff decided to file yet another lawsuit against JLF Defendants in Rhode Island Superior Court on May 22, 2022. Plaintiff once again claimed entitlement to settlement funds that he believed he was owed because of his representation of JLF Defendants’ clients. JLF Defendants removed this case to the U.S. District Court for the District of Rhode Island based on diversity jurisdiction. JLF Defendants moved to transfer venue to the Fort Worth Division of the Northern District of Texas based on certain provisions within their engagement agreement with Moreno. The District of Rhode Island granted the JLF Defendants’ motion and ordered that the case be transferred to the Northern District of Texas, Fort Worth Division, where it was assigned to the undersigned on May 3, 2023.

This Court entered a Memorandum Opinion and Order, ECF No. 94, and Final Judgment, ECF No. 95, dismissing Plaintiff’s claims with prejudice on the grounds of res judicata. The Court dismissed “all claims and causes of action asserted against” JLF Defendants and “all other claims or causes of action which could have been asserted by” Plaintiff.

On June 21, 2025, a year after this Court entered Final Judgment dismissing all claims, Plaintiff filed a Motion to Disburse in Rhode Island Superior Court against Epiq Global (formerly, Garretson Resolution Group, “GRG”). JLF Defendants intervened alleging that Plaintiff’s claims in the Motion to Disburse in Rhode Island had already been adjudicated by the Texas arbitration of 2024. To stop Plaintiff, JLF Defendants ask this Court to exercise its equitable powers and enjoin both Plaintiff from bringing his Motion to Disburse in Rhode Island Superior Court and the Rhode Island Superior Court from deciding his Motion (ECF No. 96). JLF Defendants also ask this Court to send any further claims to arbitration. The Motions are now ripe for this Court’s review.

II. LEGAL STANDARD
A. Anti-Injunction Act, 28 U.S.C. § 2283.

Since its inception, our nation has operated under a dual system of federal and state courts.
Yet, an inherent tension exists between them just by nature of their parallel functions. Chick Kam Choo v. Exxon Corp., 486 U.S. 140, 146 (1988). Although federal courts possess authority to “issue all writs necessary or appropriate in aid of their respective jurisdictions[,]” 28 U.S.C.
§ 1651, to prevent federal courts from encroaching upon state court matters, the Judiciary Act of 1793 recognized that no “writ of injunction [shall] be granted to stay proceedings in any court of state.” Act of Mar. 2, 1793, ch. 22, § 5, 1 Stat. 335. Later, in 1948, Congress formally codified this principle under 28 U.S.C. § 2283—known as the Anti-Injunction Act—to promote and preserve a balance between judicial systems. Chick Kam Choo, 486 U.S. at 146; 28 U.S.C. § 2283. At its Case 4:23-cv-00415-O Document 110 Filed 09/14/26 Page 4 of 12 PageID <pageID>

- 5 -
core, the Act’s “message is one of respect for state courts.” Smith v. Bayer Corp., 564 U.S. 299,
306 (2011).

The text of the Anti-Injunction Act is quite clear: “A court of the United States may not grant an injunction to stay proceedings in a State court.” 28 U.S.C. § 2283 (emphasis added).
Congress did, however, carve out three exceptions for when a federal court may enjoin a state court from pursuing adjudication over a claim: (1) if it is expressly authorized by Act of Congress,
(2) where necessary in aid of its jurisdiction, and (3) to protect or effectuate a federal court’s judgments. Id. The Supreme Court directs lower courts to narrowly construe these exceptions— “not [to] be enlarged by loose statutory construction.” Chick Kam Choo, 486 U.S. at 146 (quoting Atlantic Coast Line R. Co. v. Locomotive Engineers, 389 U.S. 281, 287 (1970)). Lower courts are instructed that “any doubts as to the propriety of a federal injunction against state court proceedings should be resolved in favor of permitting the state courts to proceed.” Smith, 564 U.S. at 306 (quoting Atlantic Coast Line R. Co., 398 U.S. at 297).

Applicable here is the third exception, also known as the relitigation exception, which rests on the principles of res judicata. Vasquez v. Bridgestone/Firestone, Inc., 325 F.3d 665, 675 (5th Cir. 2003). The basic idea is that a party cannot relitigate a claim in state court once a federal court has already adjudicated the matter. Chick Kam Choo, 486 U.S. at 147 (“The relitigation exception was designed to permit a federal court to prevent state litigation of an issue that previously was presented to and decided by the federal court.”). To preserve the federal court’s judgment, under this exception, a federal court may issue an injunction barring the state court from considering the claim. Like res judicata, the relitigation exception requires that (1) the parties in the state action must be identical (or at least in privity with) the parties in the previous federal action, (2) the judgment in the prior action must have been rendered by a court of competent jurisdiction, (3) the Case 4:23-cv-00415-O Document 110 Filed 09/14/26 Page 5 of 12 PageID <pageID>

- 6 -
prior action must have concluded with a final judgment on the merits, and (4) the same claim or cause of action must be involved in both suits. New York Life Ins. Co. v. Gillispie, 203 F.3d 384,
387 (5th Cir. 2000) (quoting United States v. Shanbaum, 10 F.3d 305, 310 (5th Cir. 1994)).
Importantly, the previous claim before the federal court must have been actually decided for the exception to apply. Smith, 564 U.S. at 302 (“the issue presented in the state court [has to be] identical to the one decided in the federal tribunal”); Texas Com. Bank Nat. Ass’n v. State of Fla., 138 F.3d 179, 182 (5th Cir. 1998) (“an issue must be ‘actually litigated’ in federal court before an injunction can issue under the relitigation exception”). For example, in Chick Kam Choo the Supreme Court found that the relitigation exception did not apply because the federal court’s order rested its analysis on forum non conveniens grounds. Chick Kam Choo, 486 U.S. at 150. The state court was not barred from hearing the case simply because the federal court had deemed the claims nonjusticiable. Id. Because the federal court had not decided the claims on the merits, it became the state court’s responsibility to decide whether the claim before them was reviewable.
Id. at 151.

The same principle can be applied to the doctrine of preclusion. When a court applies the preclusion doctrines of res judicata or collateral estoppel, it does not evaluate, consider, nor adjudicate the underlying claims. Rather the court asks whether the claim itself has been litigated before. A decision on the merits answers the substance of the dispute, whereas a nonjusticiable ruling is a determination preventing the court from reaching those claims in the first place.
Applying Chick Kam Choo to the question of preclusion, a federal court may not enjoin a state court when the federal court’s previous order rested its judgment on preclusion grounds; so, it is up to the “the state court [to] decide the preclusion question.” Smith, 564 U.S. at 318.
Case 4:23-cv-00415-O Document 110 Filed 09/14/26 Page 6 of 12 PageID <pageID>

- 7 -
B. All Writs Act, 28 U.S.C. § 1561.

The All Writs Act, an equitable instrument, allows federal courts to “issue all writs necessary or appropriate” to aid their jurisdiction and to protect their judgments. 28 U.S.C. § 1651.
“[B]ecause federal courts, being courts of limited jurisdiction, would not otherwise possess the tools necessary to implement their jurisdictional grants[,]” the All Writs Act is a powerful tool possessed by the federal courts. ITT Community Dev. Corp. v. Barton, 569 F.2d 1351, 1359 (5th Cir. 1978). The origins of the All Writs Act can be traced back to legal principles that predate the founding, and like the Anti-Injunction Act, the All Writs Act can be found in founding era legislation. See Jud. Act 1789, § 14. This law was later codified by Congress in 28 U.S.C. § 1651 (“The Supreme Court and all courts established by Act of Congress may issue all writs necessary or appropriate in aid of their respective jurisdictions and agreeable to the usage and principles of law.”).

The Fifth Circuit has instructed that the “scope of . . . the All Writs Act depends on the nature of the case before the court and the legitimacy of the ends sought to be achieved through the exercise of the power.” In re Lease Oil Antitrust Litigation No. II, 48 F.Supp.2d 699, 703 (S.D.
Tex. 1998) (quoting ITT Community Dev. Corp. v. Barton, 569 F.2d 1351, 1358–59 (5th Cir.
1978)) (cleaned up). Like the Anti-Injunction Act exceptions, any use of the All Writs Act by a court must be applied narrowly. United States v. New York Telephone Co., 434 U.S. 159, 172–73 (1977). Specifically, any injunction issued via the All Writs Act shall be “directed at conduct which, left unchecked, would have had the practical effect of diminishing the court’s power to bring the litigation to its natural conclusion.” Barton, 569 F.2d at 1358–59. Thus, if a litigant’s Case 4:23-cv-00415-O Document 110 Filed 09/14/26 Page 7 of 12 PageID <pageID>

- 8 -
conduct is “not shown to be detrimental to the court’s jurisdiction or exercise thereof” the litigant cannot be enjoined by a court under the Act. Id. at 1359.

III. ANALYSIS
A. The Anti-Injunction Act’s Re-Litigation Exception Does Not Apply Because this Court Did Not Adjudicate the Claim on the Merits.

JLF Defendants urge this Court to apply the relitigation exception of the Anti-Injunction Act to enjoin Rhode Island Superior Court from adjudicating the Motion to Disburse filed by Deaton in June 2025.

5
JLF Defendants argue that because Plaintiff’s claims have already been adjudicated during the Texas arbitration and because this Court dismissed Plaintiff’s previous claim on res judiciata grounds, that the relitigation exception applies.6 Plaintiff does not deny that he brought claims against JLF Defendants in this Court in 2022.7 Plaintiff instead argues that the relitigation exception does not apply because this Court did not actually adjudicate the claim on the merits.8 The Court agrees with the Plaintiff.

JLF Defendants have not established that all four elements of the relitigation exception of the Anti-Injunction Act are met. JLF Defendants had to establish that (1) the parties in the state action are identical (or at least in privity with) the parties in the previous federal action, (2) the judgment in the prior action was rendered by a court of competent jurisdiction, (3) the prior action concluded with a final judgment on the merits, and (4) the same claim or cause of action was involved in both suits. New York Life Ins. Co. v. Gillispie, 203 F.3d 384, 387 (5th Cir. 2000) (quoting United States v. Shanbaum, 10 F.3d 305, 310 (5th Cir. 1994)).

1. The first, second, and fourth elements have been met.

5 Defs.’ Mot. Reopen Case and Injunctive Relief, Ex. A (Motion to Order Equip Global, As Successor to Garretson Resolution Group to Disburse the March 11, 2016 Lein), App. 2–19, ECF No. 97-1.
6 Defs.’ Mot. Reopen Case and Injunctive Relief 14, ECF No. 97.

7 Pl.’s Br. Resp. Mot. Reopen Case and Injunctive Relief 3, ECF No. 103.

8
Id. at 5.

Case 4:23-cv-00415-O Document 110 Filed 09/14/26 Page 8 of 12 PageID <pageID>

- 9 -
As to the first element, JLF Defendants demonstrate that the parties in the state action are in privity with the parties in the previous federal action. Plaintiff and JLF Defendants were named parties in the federal action brought before this Court in 2022.9In the current state action before Rhode Island Superior Court, Plaintiff once again brings suit, but this time against Eqip Global, a third-party settlement fund administrator.10 As JLF Defendants note, Eqip Global is merely a “nominal party.”11 Accordingly, “[c]ourts look beyond the nominal parties, and treat all those whose interests are involved in the litigation and who conduct and control the action or defense as real parties, and hold them concluded by any judgment which may be rendered.” Southern Cnty.
Mut. Ins. Co. v. Ochoa, 19 S.W.3d 452, 464 (Tex. App. Ct. 2000). Here, the money held by Eqip Global is of interest to JLF Defendants. So, the first element is met.

The second element is met because the Arbitration Award was issued by a court of competent jurisdiction. The 48th Judicial District of Tarrant County, Texas referred Plaintiff and JLF Defendants to arbitration pursuant to crossclaims by both parties including breach of contract related to their fee-sharing agreement. JLF Defendants point out that Plaintiff’s claims were already actually litigated or could have been litigated during the Texas Arbitration. Deaton v.
Johnson, No. 4:23-CV-00415-O, 2024 WL 920082, at *4 (N.D. Tex. Mar. 4, 2024). So, a final judgment was issued by a court of competent jurisdiction.

JLF Defendants meet the fourth element because the same claims are involved in both suits.
Plaintiff has been attempting to circumvent res judiciata principles by filing the same causes of action in different forums. Plaintiff has attempted to relitigate the breach of contract claim and the validity of the Arbitration Award more than once. This Court dismissed those claims because they
9 Deaton v. Johnson, No. 4:23-CV-00415-O, 2024 WL 920082 (N.D. Tex. Mar. 4, 2024).
10 Defs.’ Mot. Reopen Case and Injunctive Relief, Ex. A (Motion to Order Equip Global, As Successor to Garretson Resolution Group to Disburse the March 11, 2016 Lein), App. 2–19, ECF No. 97-1.
11 Defs.’ Mot. Reopen Case and Injunctive Relief 18, ECF No. 97.

Case 4:23-cv-00415-O Document 110 Filed 09/14/26 Page 9 of 12 PageID <pageID>

- 10 -
had already been litigated and a final judgment entered by the Texas state courts. Deaton, No.
4:23-CV-00415-O, 2024 WL 920082, at *5.

2. JLF Defendants’ claim falls short as to the third element.

It is the third element that bars this court from enjoining Rhode Island Superior Court from deciding Plaintiff’s present Motion to Disburse. The Supreme Court has ordered lower courts to “assess[] the precise state of the record and what the earlier federal order actually said; it [does] not permit the District Court to render a post hoc judgment as to what the order was intended to say.” Chick Kam Choo, 486 U.S. at 148 (citing Atl. Coast Line R. Co., 398 U.S. 281) (emphasis in original). Turning to this Court’s previous Order, this Court dismissed with prejudice all of Plaintiff’s claims on res judiciata grounds, concluding that the Texas Final Arbitration Award precluded its review of Plaintiff’s claims because a final judgment on the merits had been rendered by a court of competent jurisdiction. Deaton, No. 4:23-CV-00415-O, 2024 WL 920082. The claims on the merits was not addressed.

Presently, Plaintiff’s Motion to Disburse attempts to once again challenge the validity of the Texas Final Arbitration Award of 2024 to get another bite at the apple, but this Court’s hands are tied. Since this Court did not actually adjudicate the underlying merits of the claims, this Court does not have the authority to apply the relitigation exception. Chick Kam Choo, 486 U.S. at 148 (“an essential prerequisite for applying the relitigation exception is that the claims or issues which the federal injunction insulates from litigation in state proceedings actually have been decided by the federal court”). As Atlantic Coast instructs lower courts, this element must be held to a “strict and narrow” standard. Id.; see generally Atl. Coast Line R. Co., 398 U.S. 281.

It is the responsibility of JLF Defendants to urge the Rhode Island Superior Court to give full faith and credit to the Texas Arbitration Order of 2024. Therefore, JLF Defendants’ Motion to Case 4:23-cv-00415-O Document 110 Filed 09/14/26 Page 10 of 12 PageID <pageID>

- 11 -
Enjoin the Rhode Island Superior Court from Adjudicating Plaintiff’s Motion to Disburse is
DENIED.

B. Enjoining Plaintiff Under the All Writs Act is Unwarranted.

Plaintiff’s actions do not “diminish[] the [C]ourt’s power to bring the litigation to its natural conclusion” because the question presently before the Rhode Island Superior Court was not litigated nor decided by this Court. Barton, 569 F.2d at 1358–59. JLF Defendants claim that Plaintiff, a vexatious litigant, brings the state court action to relitigate matters already decided.12 Additionally, they ask this Court to require Plaintiff to “withdraw his Motion to Disburse with prejudice” and to “prevent[] him from taking any position in any future litigation” that is contrary to the Texas Arbitration Award.13 Plaintiff’s actions do not disturb this Court’s judgment. Barton,
569 F.2d at 1358–59. Plaintiff’s Motion to Disburse calls into question the validity, perhaps, of the Final Arbitration Award entered by the Texas state court. However, it is the Rhode Island Superior Court’s duty to decide whether the claims before it have already been litigated such that res judiciata applies. Because the All Writs Act must be applied narrowly, we refuse to enjoin Plaintiff.

For Plaintiff to be deemed a vexatious litigant, the Fifth Circuit requires the JLF Defendants to proffer clear and convincing evidence that “every facet of [this] litigation was patently meritless” as well as evidence of Plaintiff’s “bad faith, improper motive, or reckless disregard of the duty owed to the court.” Bryant v. Mil. Dep’t of Mississippi, 597 F.3d 678, 694 (5th Cir. 2010) (quoting Procter & Gamble Co. v. Amway Corp., 280 F.3d 519, 525–26 (5th Cir. 2002) (emphasis in original)).

12 Defs.’ Mot. Reopen Case and Injunctive Relief 27, ECF No. 97.

13 Id.

Case 4:23-cv-00415-O Document 110 Filed 09/14/26 Page 11 of 12 PageID <pageID>

- 12 -
This Court is not persuaded that the evidence presented meets the considerably high burden demanded by the Fifth Circuit precedent to merit an injunction against Plaintiff and to deem him a vexatious litigant. Accordingly, the Court concludes that all relief prayed in JLF Defendants’ Motion to Enjoin Deaton should be DENIED.

C. The Court Refuses to Order Parties to Arbitration.

Additionally, JLF Defendants ask this Court to order arbitration for any conceivable remaining disputes. Because this Court’s order of March 3, 2024 concluded that any claims or disputes that have been brought or could have been brought by Plaintiff during the Texas Arbitration were barred from litigation, the Court does not find it necessary to order parties to arbitrate. Once again, it is Rhode Island Superior Court’s responsibility to decide whether the case before them is barred by res judicata such that the claim must be dismissed, or that there are remaining claims that must be adjudicated such that arbitration may be warranted. Therefore, JLF Defendants’ Motion to Order Arbitration is DENIED.

IV. CONCLUSION
It is hereby ORDERED that JLF Defendants’ Motion to Reopen this Matter and for Injunctive Relief, or Alternatively, Application for Arbitration (ECF No. 96) is DENIED.
The Court reminds Plaintiff of its warning from the Court’s March 4, 2024, Order (ECF No. 94). Once again, Mr. John Deaton is hereby WARNED that his filing of any additional lawsuits in the Northern District of Texas, Fort Worth Division, which are of a similarly duplicative or antagonistic nature SHALL result in either a monetary sanction assessed against him, or an injunction restraining his capacity to file or otherwise appear in any prospective lawsuit in the Fort Worth Division without the preclearance of the District Court, or both.
SO ORDERED on this 14th day of September, 2026.

(“DUAL”), Chaucer Syndicate 1084 of Lloyd’s (“Chaucer”), and Houston Casualty Company (“Houston”) (collectively, “Defendants”), for declaratory judgment and breach of contract. Before the Court is Defendants’ Motion to Transfer Venue Under 28 U.S.C. § 1404(a) (Doc. 13), DUAL’s Motion to Dismiss Pursuant to F.R.C.P. 12(b)(6) (Doc. 10), and DUAL’s Request for Hearing on Motion to Dismiss (Doc. 19). The motions are fully briefed, and the Court is prepared to rule. For the reasons explained below, the Court denies each motion.

The following facts are alleged in Plaintiff’s Complaint or included in the insurance policy attached to the Complaint.

Plaintiff is a full-service logistics solutions provider that offers a wide range of freight transportation services. In February 2025, Plaintiff purchased contingent cargo insurance from Defendants for the policy period of February 23, 2025 to

II. Motion to Transfer Venue
Section 32 of the Policy contains a forum-selection clause that provides in relevant part:
Any suit, action or proceeding arising out of or based upon this policy shall be instituted in the United States District Court for the Southern District of New York or the United States District Court in the district in which this policy was issued. Each party to this contract submits to the exclusive jurisdiction of such courts in any suit, action or proceeding and waives any objection to venue of any suit, action or proceeding in the said courts.11
A mandatory forum-selection clause is properly enforced by a motion to transfer pursuant to 28 U.S.C. § 1404(a).

12 Generally, when considering a motion under § 1404(a), the Court reviews several factors, including the Plaintiff’s choice of forum.13 But in the case of a

Marine Constr. Co. v. U.S. Dist. Ct. for W.
Dist. of Tex., 571 U.S. 49, 59, 61 (2013) (“[Section] 1406(a) and Rule 12(b)(3) are not proper mechanisms to enforce a forum-selection clause and . . . § 1404(a) and the forum non conveniens doctrine provide appropriate enforcement mechanisms.”).

The parties agree that New York law governs this contract dispute under the choice-oflaw provision in the Policy. Indeed, in a diversity action such as this, the Court must apply the choice-of-law rules of the state in which it sits.17 With certain exceptions that do not apply here, “[w]here the parties to a contract have entered an agreement that incorporates a choice of law provision, Kansas courts generally effectuate the law chosen by the parties to control the agreement.”

“issued” under N.Y. Insurance Law § 3425 is “[t]o send out or distribute officially.”29 And one dictionary defines the term as “[t]he act of circulating, distributing, or publishing.”30 Defendants summarize these authorities as confirming that “issued” is unambiguously limited to where an act by the insurer occurred, limiting the Policy to a single place of issuance.
Defendants argue that the single place the Policy was issued was San Diego, California— DUAL’s principal place of business—based on the Policy’s signature block.
However, the caselaw demonstrates that “issued” is ambiguous in the insurance context because multiple reasonable interpretations have been proposed for the term.
31
New York caselaw is not conclusive that the location where a policy is “issued” is limited to where a physical act by the insurer occurred. The court in Taggert proposed two potential locations that a policy could be “issued”: where the policy “is delivered and accepted,” or where it “is prepared and signed.”

32
The Taggert court was not forced to choose between these two interpretations because both the “prepar[ation] and sign[ing]” and “delivery to the insured” occurred in the same state—New York.

33
The facts of this case are distinguishable because the Policy was prepared and signed by DUAL in San Diego, California, but it was delivered and accepted by Plaintiff in Overland Park, Kansas.

34

29 Rosner v. Metro. Prop. & Liab. Ins., 754 N.E.2d 760, 764 (N.Y. 2001) (quoting Black’s Law Dictionary
836 (7th ed. 1999)).

30 The American Heritage Dictionary of English Language 931 (5th ed. 2022).
31 Indian Harbor, 972 F. Supp. 2d at 649 (“[B]oth asserted definitions—“prepared and signed” versus “sent out or distributed officially”—find some support in the case law.”)

32 Taggert, 100 N.Y.S.2d at 564.

33 See id. at 564–65.

34 Defendants argue that the Policy was also “delivered and accepted” in California because the Policy became mutually binding when it was signed by DUAL. Doc. 20 at 2–3. However, a party must accept an offer before it can be bound to its terms, and acceptance can occur either through a party’s performance or promise of future performance. Butchers’ Advocate Co. v. Berkof, 158 N.Y.S. 160, 161 (N.Y. App. Div. 1916) (finding that “a mere offer or unilateral promise on the part of the defendant, . . .could ripen into a mutual contract only when the offer was accepted and the acceptance [was] evidenced either by an express promise to perform or by performance.”). Thus, DUAL’s signature alone cannot bind Plaintiff. Instead, because there is no evidence of a Case 2:26-cv-02326-JAR-ADM Document 21 Filed 09/14/26 Page 6 of 12

7
Having determined that the ordinary meaning of “issued” in the insurance context is susceptible to two reasonable interpretations, the Court next examines whether reading it in the context of the Policy as a whole resolves the ambiguity.35 Section 32 requires the court to be “in the district in which this policy was issued.”36 The word’s surrounding language does not clarify whether “issued” means the place where the policy was sent or received. An insurance policy could be “issued by” a party in one state and “issued to” a party in another state. Without any supporting prepositions or clear subject in the sentence, it is unclear why the location of issuance should be fixed to the district in which Defendants’ actions were initiated, rather than where the results of their actions occurred.

37

Because the Court finds that Section 32 is ambiguous, the Court resolves the ambiguity in favor of Plaintiff as the insured. And while this canon of construction does not apply “in a contest between two insurance companies,”38 Plaintiff is not an insurance company. Defendants’ contention that it would be untenable to construe its own “standard-form policy” as “issued in whichever State each insured happened to occupy,” is unavailing since Section 32 still limits the potential forums to two locations—the Southern District of New York or the location of the insured with whom Defendants chose to do business. If Defendants intended for Section 32 to prior promise of performance, Plaintiff’s acceptance is evidenced by its initial performance—paying the Policy’s premiums from its office in Overland Park, Kansas where the Policy was delivered.
35 In Rosner v. Metropolitan Property & Liability. Insurance, the Court of Appeals of New York was able to construe “the phrase ‘as of’ . . . in conjunction with the word ‘issue,’” when holding that the date of issuance was “a date mutually agreed upon by the parties having some contractual significance, rather than merely referencing the date of an event, such as the execution or delivery of a document.” 754 N.E.2d at 480 36
Doc. 1-1 at 18.

37 See Robles v. United Auto. Ins., 333 So. 3d 735, 737 (Fla. Dist. Ct. App. 2021) (“In the insurance context, the term ‘issued’ can mean different things. It can mean delivery to an insured where the insured risk is located.”)

38 Id. (quoting United States Fire Ins. v. Gen. Reinsurance Corp., 949 F.2d 569, 573 (2d Cir. 1991)).
Case 2:26-cv-02326-JAR-ADM Document 21 Filed 09/14/26 Page 7 of 12

8 permit suits only in their two preferred districts, they could have done so directly by specifically naming the preferred forum.

Therefore, because Section 32’s ambiguous language could reasonably be interpreted to permit this Court as a proper forum, the Court denies Defendants’ motion to transfer venue.
III. DUAL’s Motion to Dismiss
DUAL individually moves to dismiss Plaintiff’s claims against it pursuant to Fed. R. Civ.
P. 12(b)(6), and it separately moved for a hearing on this motion. As a preliminary matter, after considering the parties’ briefs and the relevant law, the Court denies DUAL’s motion for hearing because it would not materially assist the Court in deciding this motion. Moreover, for the reasons below, the Court denies DUAL’s Motion to Dismiss.

To survive a motion to dismiss brought under Fed. R. Civ. P. 12(b)(6), a complaint must contain factual allegations that, assumed to be true, “raise a right to relief above the speculative level”39 and must include “enough facts to state a claim for relief that is plausible on its face.”40 Under this standard, “the complaint must give the court reason to believe that this plaintiff has a reasonable likelihood of mustering factual support for these claims.”41 The plausibility standard does not require a showing of probability that “a defendant has acted unlawfully,” but requires more than “a sheer possibility.”42 “[M]ere ‘labels and conclusions,’ and ‘a formulaic recitation of the elements of a cause of action’ will not suffice; a plaintiff must offer specific factual
39 Bell Atl. Corp. v. Twombly, 550 U.S. 544, 555 (2007) (citing 5C Wright & Miller’s Federal Practice and Procedure § 1216 (3d ed. 2004)).

40 Id. at 570.

41 Ridge at Red Hawk, L.L.C. v. Schneider, 493 F.3d 1174, 1177 (10th Cir. 2007) (emphasis in original).
42 Ashcroft v. Iqbal, 556 U.S. 662, 678 (2009) (citing Twombly, 550 U.S. at 556).
Case 2:26-cv-02326-JAR-ADM Document 21 Filed 09/14/26 Page 8 of 12

9 allegations to support each claim.”43 Finally, the court must accept the nonmoving party’s factual allegations as true and may not dismiss on the ground that it appears unlikely the allegations can be proven.44
Plaintiff premises its common law breach of contract claim on diversity jurisdiction, and both parties assert without explanation that the Court must apply Kansas substantive law to this claim.

45
However, as the Court explained above, Kansas law generally applies the choice of law that the parties specified in their contract’s choice of law provision.46 Section 32 of the Policy contains a valid and enforceable choice of law provision which provides that the Policy is governed by federal maritime common law, with New York law supplementing the common law if necessary.47 Thus, consistent with the Policy, the Court will analyze Plaintiff’s claim under federal maritime common law.48
“Federal maritime law stems from the maritime jurisprudence of the federal courts, and is an amalgam of traditional common law rules, modifications of those rules, and newly created

45 See Doc. 11 at 2 (“Because this Court sits in diversity, it must apply Kansas substantive law to the claims asserted.”); Doc. 17 at 2 (applying Kansas law).

PepsiCo, Inc., 431 F.3d 1241, 1255 (10th Cir. 2005) (finding that New York law applied to a plaintiff’s breach of contract claim because the contract stated that its “terms and conditions . . . shall be governed and interpreted by New York law.”).

47 Doc. 1-1 at 18 (“This contract of marine insurance and the rights and obligations of DUAL Commercial and the Insured hereunder shall be governed and construed in accordance with federal maritime common law of the United States. In the absence of controlling federal maritime common law . . . , the law of the state of New York shall apply, irrespective of any principles of choice of law.”).

48 See Patterson v. Williams, No. 10–cv–4094, 2012 WL 138606, at *2 (D. Kan. Jan. 18, 2012) (“[T]he court is not obligated to analyze a choice of law issue sua sponte. But neither is the court prohibited from doing so.”

10 rules drawn from state and federal sources.”49 “Under the federal common law, ‘[t]he elements of a breach of contract claim are [1] the existence of a contract, [2] material breach, and [3] damages.’”

50
These elements mirror those required under Kansas and New York law.51 DUAL argues that Plaintiff cannot demonstrate the first element of breach of contract because the Policy is not an insurance contract between Plaintiff and itself, it is an insurance contract between Plaintiff and the carrier defendants, Houston and Chaucer. DUAL claims that it should not be a defendant in this case because it “was not the insurer responsible for coverage under the Policy, did not assume the carrier defendants’ contractual obligations, and owed no independent duty to the Plaintiff.”

DUAL argues that it was only an “agent” under the Policy, and that agents are only “authorized in writing, by any insurance company . . . to negotiate or effect contracts of insurance, suretyship or indemnity on behalf of any such insurance company.”53 DUAL argues that the Policy reflects its limited role, and likewise its limited liability, as an agent. The Court disagrees.

The Policy’s plain language states that DUAL is liable for claims arising under Section

UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT
SOUTHERN DISTRICT OF FLORIDA
CASE NO. 26-cv-20364-ALTMAN
RAHUL BAGGA,
Plaintiff,
v.

ROYAL CARIBBEAN CRUISES LTD.,
Defendant.

_________________________________________/
ORDER GRANTING MOTION TO DISMISS
Our Plaintiff, Rahul Bagga, has sued Royal Caribbean Cruises Ltd. (“RCCL”), asserting claims of negligent maintenance, negligent failure to warn, and negligent design. See generally Complaint [ECF No. 1]. RCCL has moved to dismiss this action “because the Ticket Contract between [Bagga] and RCCL contains a forum selection clause that requires all suits for personal injuries arising out of the subject cruise to be litigated, if at all, in and before the Courts of England and Wales.” Motion to Dismiss (the “Motion”) [ECF No. 13] at 1. The Motion is fully briefed and ripe for adjudication. See Response in Opposition to the Motion (the “Response”) [ECF No. 28]; Reply in Support of the Motion (the “Reply”) [ECF No. 29]. After careful review, we GRANT the Motion.

THE FACTS
Bagga is “a resident and citizen of Canada.” Complaint ¶ 2. “On January 22, 2025, [Bagga] was a fare paying passenger aboard the ICON[.]” Id. ¶ 11. “At all material times, including January 22, 2025, [Bagga] was participating in an onboard game for passengers sponsored and organized by RCCL, specifically the ‘Crazy Quest Adult Scavenger Hunt’ [the ‘Scavenger Hunt’] in the Absolute Zero Ice Rink on Deck 3 of the ICON.” Id. ¶ 12. “[W]ithin the rink, there was a wet, foreign and/or transitory substance on the walking surface[.]” Id. ¶ 13. During the Scavenger Hunt, an RCCL crewmember Case 1:26-cv-20364-RKA Document 30 Entered on FLSD Docket 09/14/2026 Page 1 of 12

2
“called for passenger participants to bring up items to the stage in order to receive points[.]” Id. ¶ 14.
Bagga walked to the stage as instructed, but “slipped on the wet area of the walking surface” of the rink. Ibid. Bagga, “feeling pain but initially not knowing the full extent of his injury, limped back to his designated area for the game, slipped on an additional wet area and thereby fell again.” Ibid. As a result of his falls, Bagga “was injured in and about his body and extremities, including sustaining a torn anterior cruciate ligament and meniscus.” Id. ¶ 15.

THE LAW
Forum non conveniens “is an ancient common law doctrine that permits a court to decline jurisdiction over a case, even if personal jurisdiction and venue are otherwise proper, when there is a more convenient forum for the case to be litigated.” Esfeld v. Costa Crociere, S.P.A., 289 F.3d 1300, 1302 n.4 (11th Cir. 2002); see also Boyd v. Deadwood Tobacco Co., 2024 WL 940822, at *2 (S.D. Fla. Mar. 5, 2024) (Altman, J.) (“Under the doctrine of forum non conveniens [ ], a district court has the inherent power to decline to exercise jurisdiction even when venue is proper.” (cleaned up)). In a typical forum non conveniens case, “[t]he moving party must demonstrate that (1) an adequate alternative forum is available, (2) the public and private interest factors weigh in favor of dismissal, and (3) the plaintiff can reinstate his suit in the alternative forum without undue inconvenience or prejudice.” Leon v. Millon Air, Inc., 251 F.3d 1305, 1310–11 (11th Cir. 2001).

“Where there is a valid forum-selection clause, however, the forum non conveniens analysis is modified in three consequential ways. First, the plaintiff’s choice of forum merits no weight. Rather, as the party defying the forum-selection clause, the plaintiff bears the burden of establishing that dismissal for forum non conveniens is unwarranted. Second, a court should not consider arguments about the parties’ private interests and must deem the private-interest factors to weigh entirely in favor of the preselected forum. Third, a court in the contractually selected venue should not apply the law of Case 1:26-cv-20364-RKA Document 30 Entered on FLSD Docket 09/14/2026 Page 2 of 12

3 the transferor venue to which the parties waived their right, and this in turn may affect the publicinterest considerations.” Usme v. CMI Leisure Mgmt., Inc., 106 F.4th 1079, 1086 (11th Cir. 2024).
As a result of these modifications, “the Supreme Court has said that the existence of a valid and enforceable forum-selection clause is essentially dispositive in the forum non conveniens analysis.”
Ibid. (citing Atl. Marine Const. Co. v. U.S. Dist. Ct. for W. Dist. of Tex., 571 U.S. 49, 62–64 (2013)); see also GDG Acquisitions, LLC v. Government of Belize, 749 F.3d 1024, 1028 (11th Cir. 2014) (noting that “an enforceable forum-selection clause carries near-determinative weight” in the forum non conveniens analysis).

“A court may ‘consider matters outside the pleadings if presented in proper form by the parties’ in ruling on a motion to dismiss for forum non conveniens.” McGregor v. VP Recs., 2017 WL 2833444, at *3 (S.D. Fla. June 30, 2017) (Bloom, J.) (quoting MGC Commc’ns, Inc. v. BellSouth Telecomms., Inc., 146 F. Supp. 2d 1344, 1349 (S.D. Fla. 2001) (Gold, J.)); see also Turner v. Costa Crociere S.p.A., 9 F.4th 1341, 1346 (11th Cir. 2021) (holding that an Italian attorney’s affidavit was properly considered on motion to dismiss for forum non conveniens). A court may also consider exhibits attached to a motion to dismiss, and “if the allegations of the complaint about a particular exhibit conflict with the contents of the exhibit itself, the exhibit controls.” Hoefling v. City of Miami, 811 F.3d 1271, 1277 (11th Cir. 2016).
ANALYSIS
RCCL asks us to dismiss this case “based on the doctrine of forum non conveniens pursuant to a forum selection clause in the applicable Canada Cruise Ticket Contract (‘Ticket Contract’) accepted by [Bagga] prior to the sailing.” Motion at 2. According to RCCL, the Ticket Contract’s forumselection clause requires all suits for personal injuries arising out of the subject cruise to be litigated, if at all, in and before the Courts of England and Wales. Id. at 2. RCCL has provided a copy of the Ticket Contract, which includes the following forum-selection clause:

ALL DISPUTES, CLAIMS OR OTHER MATTER OF ANY DESCRIPTION
ARISING OUT OF, IN CONNECTION WITH OR IN ANY WAY RELATED
Case 1:26-cv-20364-RKA Document 30 Entered on FLSD Docket 09/14/2026 Page 3 of 12

4
OR INCIDENT TO THIS AGREEMENT, THE CRUISE, CRUISETOUR, LAND
TOUR OR TRANSPORT (INCLUDING A CLAIM FOR PERSONAL INJURY,
ILLNESS OR DEATH OF A GUEST) MUST BE LITIGATED, IF AT ALL, IN
AND BEFORE THE COURTS OF ENGLAND AND WALES, TO THE
EXCLUSION OF ALL OTHER COURTS OR TRIBUNALS.

Ticket Contract [ECF No. 13-2] at 4.

Bagga’s Response doesn’t dispute that he entered into the Ticket Contract, argue that the Ticket Contract is invalid, or claim that the Ticket Contract didn’t contain the relevant forum-selection clause. See generally Response; see also Reply at 2 (“Plaintiff’s Response in Opposition [ ] does not dispute that he accepted the Ticket Contract during online check-in, does not dispute that the Ticket Contract’s forum selection clause designating the Courts of England and Wales was reasonably communicated to him, and does not allege fraud or overreaching in the clause’s formation.”).1 Instead, Bagga argues that we should override the forum-selection clause because litigating in England and Wales would be inconvenient for him and may limit his potential recovery. See Response at 2 (“While the [C]ourts of England and Wales are no doubt competent to adjudicate civil disputes in general, the forum selection clause at issue requires a Canadian citizen and resident not merely to litigate in a country foreign to him, under a foreign body of law, but also to cross the Atlantic Ocean to litigate his claims.”). We’re not persuaded.

1 Bagga’s Complaint initially alleges that, “in the operative ticket contract, RCCL requires fare-paying passengers, such as [Bagga], to bring any lawsuit against it arising out of injuries or events occurring on the cruise voyage in this federal judicial district.” Complaint at 7. But RCCL has provided a copy of the Ticket Contract—which contains a forum-selection clause requiring disputes to be litigated in the Courts of England and Wales. See Ticket Contract at 4. And, as we’ve said, “if the allegations of the complaint about a particular exhibit conflict with the contents of the exhibit itself, the exhibit controls.” Hoefling, 811 F.3d at 1277. In any event, Bagga appears to concede that he was mistaken in his initial claim that the Ticket Contract contained a forum-selection clause requiring disputes to be litigated in this District because he completely abandons that argument in his Response. See generally Response.

Case 1:26-cv-20364-RKA Document 30 Entered on FLSD Docket 09/14/2026 Page 4 of 12

5
I. The Forum-Selection Clause
Under federal maritime law, “[f]orum selection clauses ‘are presumptively valid and enforceable unless the plaintiff makes a strong showing that enforcement would be unfair or unreasonable under the circumstances.’” Turner, 9 F.4th at 1345 (quoting Rucker v. Oasis Legal Fin., L.L.C., 632 F.3d 1231, 1235 (11th Cir. 2011)). “A plaintiff can defeat this presumption by showing that (1) the clause was induced by fraud or overreaching; (2) the plaintiff would be deprived of its day in court because of inconvenience or unfairness; (3) the chosen law would deprive the plaintiff of a remedy; or (4) enforcement of the clause would contravene public policy.” Ibid. (cleaned up). Bagga invokes only the second and third exceptions.

2
a. Inconvenience
First, Bagga argues that litigating in the Courts of England and Wales would be inconvenient for him as a Canadian citizen. See Response at 3 (“In order to present even his own testimony in a hypothetical English or Welsh proceeding, [Bagga] would still have to travel in person across the Atlantic, incurring the associated air travel and hotel costs and visa procedures, or accept the suboptimal solution of a fully electronic and fully remote proceeding[.] . . . If he wished to present the testimony of other lay or expert witnesses in person, he would also have to make arrangements for and incur the costs of their travel to the United Kingdom.”). But “a plaintiff seeking to establish that a forum selection clause is unenforceable due to inconvenience or unfairness bears a heavy burden of proof.” Turner, 9 F.4th at 1345–46 (cleaned up). And a plaintiff relying on “inconveniences that were foreseeable at the time of contracting in order to meet this burden can prevail only by showing that trial in the contractual forum will be so gravely difficult and inconvenient that he will for all practical purposes be deprived of his day in court.” Id. at 1346 (cleaned up). Bagga hasn’t met this heavy burden.
2 Bagga doesn’t argue that the forum-selection clause was “induced by fraud or overreaching” or that “enforcement of the clause would contravene public policy,” Turner, 9 F.4th at 1345, so we won’t consider the applicability of those exceptions here.

Case 1:26-cv-20364-RKA Document 30 Entered on FLSD Docket 09/14/2026 Page 5 of 12

6
He doesn’t argue that the inconvenience of litigating in the Courts of England and Wales was unforeseeable at the time of contracting or demonstrate that litigating his case in the Courts England and Wales would effectively deprive him of his day in court. See generally Response (silent as to foreseeability).

The Eleventh Circuit’s decision in Turner is instructive. In that case, the Eleventh Circuit enforced an Italian forum-selection clause even though the plaintiff was a U.S. resident and the vessel had departed from a U.S. port. In so holding, the court found persuasive an affidavit from an Italian attorney explaining that the plaintiff could attend routine Italian proceedings remotely—thus obviating the plaintiff’s concerns about the inconvenience of international travel. See Turner, 9 F.4th at 1346 (“Turner still has not met his burden of proving that pursuit of his claims in Italy would subject him to fundamental unfairness. The reason is basic: he has not established that he would have to travel to Italy in order to pursue his case. The [d]efendants produced an affidavit from an Italian attorney explaining that Turner would not be required to attend routine proceedings in person and that even for those events that required attendance, he could possibly either arrange for appointment of a special attorney to attend on his behalf or request that the event take place in the United States via international rogatory.”).

So too here. RCCL has provided a declaration from an English solicitor explaining that Bagga can pursue his claims in the Courts of England and Wales, in large part, remotely. See Declaration of Milena Iyer (“Iyer Decl.”) [ECF No. 13-12] ¶ 33 (“[A]s a matter of English procedural law, it is entirely possible, for [Bagga], or any [his] fact or expert witnesses (if necessary) to give their evidence in chief remotely, should they wish to do so[.]”). And RCCL has already consented to proceeding remotely for Bagga’s convenience. See Reply at 5 (“RCCL has, moreover, expressly consented to remote proceedings, including remote presentation of witness testimony, and does not object to that format where appropriate.”). Bagga doesn’t rebut this line of argument—but simply says that proceeding Case 1:26-cv-20364-RKA Document 30 Entered on FLSD Docket 09/14/2026 Page 6 of 12

7 remotely would be “suboptimal” and that he’d prefer to “present his case in person before the judicial officer deciding his fate.” Response at 3. His preference for in-person proceedings simply isn’t sufficient to carry the “heavy burden” he bears in trying to overcome a valid forum-selection clause.
Turner, 9 F.4th at 1346.3
Even if Bagga couldn’t proceed remotely, however, he’d still have failed to show that litigating in the Courts of England and Wales would be so gravely difficult as to effectively deprive him of his day in court. As RCCL observes, “as a citizen and resident of Canada, [Bagga’s] travel to Miami requires [an] international flight and similar transit time as travel to London. Neither forum is [Bagga’s] home forum, and neither imposes a unique or extraordinary burden relative to the other.” Reply at 4.
And the other purported inconveniences Bagga identifies in his Response (the location of witnesses, the need to coordinate with foreign counsel, etc.) are just the ordinary consequences a plaintiff who agreed to a foreign forum-selection clause would face. See Lebedinsky v. MSC Cruises, S.A., 789 F. App’x 196, 202 (11th Cir. 2019) (“Lebedinsky contends that circumstances—including the continued pain she suffered in the United States from her injury, her treatment in New York, fact witnesses located in New York, and MSC Cruises’ connection to the United States—render the forum selection clause so inconvenient that it is unenforceable. But these circumstances are not the type of uncontemplated inconveniences that justify the invalidation of a forum selection clause.”).

b. Remedy
Second, Bagga advances the flimsy argument that, because the Athens Convention imposes certain liability limitations, his “recovery would be subject to a foreign statutory liability regime materially different from that available under United States maritime law.” Response at 2 (emphasis
3 Bagga cites Greco v. Nat’l Football League, 2017 WL 11679599, at *2 (N.D. Tex. Mar. 30, 2017), for the proposition that “live testimony [is] far superior to video or telephone testimony.” Response at 3. But this unpublished, magistrate judge’s decision from the Northern District of Texas has nothing to say about the enforceability of forum-selection clauses, has no precedential value, and doesn’t persuade us to ignore Turner’s endorsement of remote proceedings.

Case 1:26-cv-20364-RKA Document 30 Entered on FLSD Docket 09/14/2026 Page 7 of 12

8 added). But Bagga’s suggestion that his recovery under the Athens Convention might be different than his recovery under federal maritime law doesn’t move the needle. Courts will not “invalidate a forum selection clause simply because the remedies available in the contractually chosen forum are less favorable than those available in the courts of the United States.” Lebedinsky, 789 F. App’x at 203 (cleaned up)). “Only remedies that are so inadequate that enforcement would be fundamentally unfair render a chosen forum inadequate.” Ibid. (cleaned up).

Bagga hasn’t even tried to show that his remedies under the Athens Convention are so inadequate that they fail to meet this unfairness standard. He doesn’t tell us what damages he’s incurred, explain the liability limitations imposed by the Athens Convention, or otherwise provide any calculation from which we could compare his potential remedies under federal maritime law to his potential remedies under the Athens Convention. See generally Response. That’s not good enough— especially when it’s his burden.

The Eleventh Circuit’s decision in Lebedinsky demonstrates why Bagga’s Athens Convention argument is fatally flawed. In Lebedinsky, the plaintiff argued that she’d incurred $750,000 in damages but would be limited to a recovery of $64,000 under the Athens Convention if the relevant forumselection clause were enforced—and the Eleventh Circuit still held that this limitation on her potential recovery wasn’t enough to render the parties’ selection of Italy as a forum “fundamentally unfair.”
Lebedinsky, 789 F. App’x. at 202; see also id. at 203 (“[H]ere, the possibility of reduced recovery does not amount to fundamental unfairness, and the potential application of the Athens Convention cannot justify the forum selection clause’s invalidation.”). Bagga has done far less than Lebedinsky and therefore fails to show that his remedy under the Athens Convention would be so inadequate as to render the forum-selection clause unenforceable.

Case 1:26-cv-20364-RKA Document 30 Entered on FLSD Docket 09/14/2026 Page 8 of 12

9
II. Forum Non Conveniens
But that’s not the end of our inquiry. Having established that the parties’ forum-selection clause is enforceable, “we must now apply a modified forum non conveniens analysis and assess (1) whether an adequate alternative forum is available, (2) whether the public factors weigh in favor of dismissal, and (3) whether the plaintiff can reinstate his suit in the alternative forum without undue inconvenience or prejudice.” Boyd, 2024 WL 940822, at *8 (cleaned up)). All three of these modified factors favor RCCL.

First, the Courts of England and Wales are an adequate alternative forum for this dispute. See M/S Bremen v. Zapata Off-Shore Co., 407 U.S. 1, 12 (1972) (“Plainly, the courts of England meet the standards of neutrality and long experience in admiralty litigation.”). RCCL’s English solicitor attests to the independence and fairness of the Courts of England and Wales and tells us that “many parties to maritime disputes choose to litigate in England and Wales as a neutral jurisdiction with a specialist Admiralty Court[.] . . . As a result, a vast number of personal injury disputes arising from onboard ships have been heard in the Courts of England and Wales over the years.” Iyer Decl. ¶ 37. Bagga never argues that the Courts of England and Wales wouldn’t be an adequate alterative forum—indeed, he concedes that “the [C]ourts of England and Wales are no doubt competent to adjudicate civil disputes in general[.]” Response at 2. The first factor thus favors dismissal.

Second, the public-interest factors likewise favor dismissal. The relevant public-interest factors include “(1) the administrative difficulties flowing from court congestion; (2) the local interest in having localized controversies decided at home; (3) the interest in having the trial . . . in a forum that is at home with the law that must govern the action; (4) the avoidance of unnecessary problems in conflict of laws, or in the application of foreign law; and (5) the unfairness of burdening citizens in an unrelated forum with jury duty.” Boyd, 2024 WL 940822, at *9 (cleaned up) (citing Piper Aircraft Co. v.
Reyno, 454 U.S. 235, 241 n.6 (1981)). When weighing these factors, “the burden is on the plaintiff to Case 1:26-cv-20364-RKA Document 30 Entered on FLSD Docket 09/14/2026 Page 9 of 12

10 show that dismissal of the complaint is unwarranted.” Pappas v. Kerzner Int’l Bahamas Ltd., 585 F. App’x 962, 964 (11th Cir. 2014).

Bagga doesn’t even try to meet his burden of showing that the public-interest factors favor litigating this case in Florida. See generally Response (silent as to public-interest factors). RCCL, on the other hand, claims that “[t]his dispute has little, if any, connection to the Southern District of Florida:
[Bagga] is a citizen and resident of Canada, the Icon of the Seas is a Bahamas-flagged vessel owned by a Liberian entity, and the incident is alleged to have occurred while the vessel was in navigable waters of the Western Caribbean, not in Florida.” Reply at 7. RCCL also tells us that “the Ticket Contract’s choice-of-law clause designat[es] English law” as governing the parties’ dispute—a contention Bagga doesn’t dispute. Motion at 7; see also Iyer Decl. ¶ 10 (“The [T]icket [C]ontract was subject to an exclusive English law and jurisdiction of the Courts of England and Wales clause [ ] otherwise known as a forum selection and choice of law clause.”); id. ¶ 11 (attaching choice-of-law clause specifying that English law will govern all disputes arising from or related to the Ticket Contract).
After careful consideration, we agree with RCCL that the balance of public-interest factors weigh in favor of dismissal. First, even though neither party provided information regarding the comparative administrative congestion between the Courts of England and Wales and the Southern District of Florida, we know that the Southern District of Florida is one of the busiest and most congested court systems in the country. See Gordon v. Sandal Resorts Int’l, Ltd., 418 F. Supp. 3d 1132, 1142 (S.D. Fla. 2019) (Scola, J.) (“[T]he Southern District of Florida has one of the busiest dockets in the country[.]”). It’s therefore unlikely that Bagga would face more congestion in England than in Florida. See Aviation One of Fla., Inc. v. Airborne Ins. Consultants, 722 F. App’x 870, 885 (11th Cir. 2018) (affirming dismissal where district court found that “it was unlikely that [the appellant] would face more congestion in England than in Florida”). Second, the “local interest in having localized controversies decided at home,” id. at 885, is at best neutral, since neither party resides in Florida and Case 1:26-cv-20364-RKA Document 30 Entered on FLSD Docket 09/14/2026 Page 10 of 12

11
Bagga’s injuries didn’t occur here. Third, the Courts of England and Wales are undoubtedly more familiar than we are with English law, which will govern this dispute. See ibid. (“[T]he court determined that the last factor favored litigation in England because the retainer agreement included a choice-oflaw provision designating English law, with which English courts would obviously be more familiar.
Based on this analysis, the court concluded that the public-interest factors failed to overcome the weight of the forum-selection clause, making dismissal appropriate.”). Fourth, the Courts of England and Wales have a strong public interest in hearing cases the parties have agreed to litigate in those courts. See Jiangsu Hongyuan Pharm. Co. v. DI Glob. Logistics Inc., 159 F. Supp. 3d 1316, 1328 (S.D. Fla.
2016) (Gayles, J.) (“China has a stronger interest in having this dispute litigated in its courts, as the parties contracted to litigate in China[.]”). And fifth, RCCL’s English solicitor tells us that “trials of civil claims in England and Wales, including personal injury claims, take place before a judge sitting without a jury,” thus obviating any concern we might have about burdening an English jury with this dispute. Iyer Decl. ¶ 18. For the foregoing reasons, and because Bagga hasn’t advanced any argument on the public-interest factors, we find that the public-interest factors favor dismissal.
Finally, we find that Bagga “can reinstate his suit in the alternative forum without undue inconvenience or prejudice,” Leon, 251 F.3d at 1311, because “RCCL has agreed to waive any statuteof-limitations defense arising from the Ticket Contract’s one-year suit-filing deadline, provided Plaintiff refiles his action in the Courts of England and Wales within six months of any order of dismissal,” Reply at 7–8.

* * * *
The upshot is that “the existence of a valid and enforceable forum-selection clause is essentially dispositive in the forum non conveniens analysis.” Usme, 106 F.4th at 1086. To overcome the forum-selection clause, Bagga bore the burden “to show that dismissal of the complaint is unwarranted” based on certain public interest factors. See Pappas, 585 F. App’x at 964. He didn’t carry Case 1:26-cv-20364-RKA Document 30 Entered on FLSD Docket 09/14/2026 Page 11 of 12

12 that burden. He didn’t even try. Bagga’s Response addressed only the enforceability of the forumselection clause and never meaningfully addressed forum non conveniens. See Response at 2 (mentioning “forum non conveniens” only once). This failure warrants dismissal.

CONCLUSION
Because the parties agreed to an enforceable forum-selection clause requiring disputes to be litigated in the Courts of England and Wales, we dismiss Bagga’s Complaint under the doctrine of forum non conveniens. Accordingly, we ORDER and ADJUDGE as follows:

1. The Motion to Dismiss [ECF No. 13] is GRANTED.

2. The Complaint [ECF No. 1] is DISMISSED without prejudice.

3. The Clerk is directed to CLOSE this case. All pending deadlines are TERMINATED and any pending motions are DENIED as moot.

DONE AND ORDERED in the Southern District of Florida on September 14, 2026.

ROY K. ALTMAN
UNITED STATES DISTRICT JUDGE
cc: counsel of record

Megalomedia v. Philadelphia Indemnity Insurance Co., Fifth Circuit Appeal
