Aug. 11, 2023), and Sotloff v. Qatar Charity, 674 F. Supp. 3d 1279 (S.D. Fla. 2023) Case 1:24-cv-00206-ABJ Document 108 Filed 09/15/26 Page 45 of 51

46 were explicitly directed at the United States. See Galloway, 2023 WL 5183204, at *10–11 (discussing alleged conspiracy involving U.S.-based loans); Sotloff, 674 F. Supp. 3d at 1308–11 (discussing execution involving video published in United States stating violence was targeted at the United States). And cases such as Flag Co. v. Maynard, 376 F. Supp. 2d 849, 855–56 (N.D.
Ill. 2005), and Rudersdal v. Harris, 2022 WL 263568, at *12 (S.D.N.Y. Jan. 28, 2022), are inapposite because foreign defendants there took acts directly in the United States or were connected to a separate conspiracy involving U.S. acts.

Further, other district courts in this district have largely rejected asserting personal jurisdiction under Rule 4(k)(2) based on a conspiracy theory. See, e.g., Ofisi v. Al Shamal Islamic Bank, 2019 WL 1255096, at *5 n.8 (D.D.C. Mar. 19, 2019) (collecting cases); Aljabri v. Saud, 2022 WL 4598519, at *8 n.4 (D.D.C. Sept. 30, 2022), vacated on other grounds by 106 F.4th 1157 (D.C. Cir. 2024); Peters v. Est. of Muammar Mohammad Abu Minyar Qadhafi, 2024 WL 4603907, at *9 & n.6 (D.D.C. Aug. 28, 2004) (rejecting theory of personal jurisdiction under Rule 4(k)(2) and conspiracy) (collecting cases).

Plaintiffs also assert that Besson waived service of the complaint and therefore consented to jurisdiction under Rule 4(k)(1). Pls. Opp. 32–34. However, as explained with respect to Badal,
18 U.S.C. § 1965(b) only applies to defendants “residing in any other district,” not foreign defendants, and requires proper service. Besson explicitly reserved all defenses to the complaint.
ECF No. 44 at 4 & n.1. Plaintiffs’ reliance on Bayshore Capital Advisors, LLC v. Creative Wealth Media Finance Corp., 667 F. Supp. 3d 83, 120–22 (S.D.N.Y. 2023), is unavailing; that case involved defendants with direct ties to the United States, unlike Besson.

Case 1:24-cv-00206-ABJ Document 108 Filed 09/15/26 Page 46 of 51

47
Accordingly, plaintiffs’ allegations fail to establish that Besson had the requisite contacts with the United States or knowledge of forum-directed acts by co-conspirators, and the Court cannot exercise personal jurisdiction over him.

III. The complaint fails to state a claim that defendant Vidino was a member of the alleged RICO conspiracy.

Defendant Vidino, the American journalist over whom the Court has personal jurisdiction, is charged in only one count: Count Three, which alleges that all of the defendants conspired to violate the Racketeer Influenced and Corrupt Organizations Act, or RICO. Am. Compl. ¶¶ 319–
23. Putting aside defendants’ arguments that both the conspiracy and substantive RICO claims should be dismissed because at bottom, this is a defamation case, and plaintiffs have not alleged the necessary predicate criminal acts to invoke RICO, see UAE Mot. at 20–28; Alp Defs.’ Mot. at 21–31; Vidino Mot. at 26–44; Besson Mot. at 14–20, the Court finds that the complaint does not include facts to give rise to a plausible inference that Vidino was a member of whatever alleged conspiracy there may have been.

16
Section 1962(d) of the RICO statute, 18 U.S.C. § 1962(d), provides that it is “unlawful for any person to conspire to violate” a substantive RICO provision. As the D.C. Circuit explained in RSM Prod. Corp. v. Freshfields Bruckhaus Deringer U.S. LLP, to state a claim based on a section 1962(d) conspiracy, “the complaint must allege that (1) two or more people agreed to commit a [RICO] subsection (c) offense, and (2) a defendant agreed to further that endeavor.” 682 F.3d 1043, 1047–48 (D.C. Cir. 2012), citing Salinas v. United States, 522 U.S. 52, 65 (1997). A defendant does not have to agree to be the one who commits the predicate acts, and his liability does not depend on his personal participation in the operation of management of the alleged
16 This means that the Court need not reach Vidino’s objection to the claim on First Amendment grounds.

Case 1:24-cv-00206-ABJ Document 108 Filed 09/15/26 Page 47 of 51

48 enterprise. RSM at 1048, citing Salinas, 522 U.S. at 65–66, and United States v. Wilson, 605 F.3d 985, 1019 (D.C. Cir. 2010). But the complaint must include facts to support a finding that the defendant himself “adopt[ed] the goal of furthering or facilitating the criminal endeavor.” Salinas,
522 U.S. at 65.

The complaint here contains nothing of the sort. In their opposition to the motion to dismiss, plaintiffs point to paragraph 40 of the amended complaint, but it says nothing about Vidino’s knowing agreement to pursue the alleged co-conspirators’ unlawful goal.
Vidino was hired by Alp as a contractor to provide leads on new targets and research and analysis on the Muslim Brotherhood. Alp routinely directed Vidino to obtain information it could then use in connection with its viral communication campaigns. He typically was paid between $2,000–$4,000 for each discreet assignment he completed for Alp. . . . Vidino routinely communicated with Brero, Badal, and another Alp employee via WhatsApp.

***
The enterprise also funneled information to Vidino and relied on him, and his academic credentials, to legitimize the false and misleading statements the enterprise published to discredit, disparage, and destroy its targets. . . . When Alp uncovered . . . Facebook posts, it passed them on to Vidino and told the UAE that Alp ‘channeled our findings to the academic expert Lorenzo Vidino,’ who shared them with a journalist at The Times.

Alp noted that using Vidino as an intermediary ensured that Alp would remain ‘completely confidential.’

Am. Compl. ¶¶ 40–41; see also Am Compl. ¶ 126 (alleging Badal and Brero “intended to use Vidino as an American analogue to Besson – a credible and highly-credentialed intermediary who was willing to say whatever they wanted in exchange for money”); ¶ 129 (“On January 24, 2018, Vidino signed a contract with Alp to provide ‘[i]nteresting leads/rumours . . . regarding the subject of investigation organisations/individuals/funding in Europe’ and a ‘[l]ist of alleged members of the first tier organisations in European countries.’ Alp agreed to pay Vidino 3,000 Euros for this Case 1:24-cv-00206-ABJ Document 108 Filed 09/15/26 Page 48 of 51

49 work.”); ¶ 186 (“To add a thin veneer of legitimacy to spurious claims, [Alp’s] Wikipedia entries quoted Lorenzo Vidino.”); ¶ 281 (quoting an article that appeared in the German newspaper Der Spiegel in 2023 reporting that “[a]n Italian American scholar named Lorenzo Vidino played an important role in the campaign . . . Since 2018, he has repeatedly performed work for Alp Services . . . .”). Even if one reads these factual assertions in the light most favorable to the plaintiffs, they may say something about Alp’s state of mind, but they do not reveal anything about Vidino’s.

Plaintiffs’ opposition maintains that Vidino “met with Alp employees on multiple occasions, including at a lavish dinner in Geneva in January 2018,” and “routinely exchanged WhatsApp messages with Brero, Badal, and another Alp employee,” and that “the volume and frequency of Vidino’s communications with Alp evince a closeness that provides evidence of Vidino’s agreement to further the enterprise’s racketeering activities.” Pls.’ Opp. at 67. But plaintiffs’ rhetoric is not supported by their own complaint, even as it has been amended. There are allegations about exactly one meeting, the dinner, see Am. Compl. ¶ 128, and the allegedly “routine” WhatsApp communications are not quoted in any other paragraph of the complaint. Am.
Compl. ¶ 40. This is not enough to add up to knowing consent to advance the goals of the alleged co-conspirators. Vidino’s alleged expression in a WhatsApp message after the fact of “his belief that Credit Suisse withdrew its line of credit to Lord Energy because of an article the enterprise had published,” Am. Compl. ¶ 40, does not serve that purpose either.

Nor does the complaint supply facts to support an inference that Vidino knew that the point of his work for Alp was to advance the goals of the UAE. The best plaintiffs could come up was a statement Vidino made after the fact to the author of an article in The New Yorker in 2024 that Case 1:24-cv-00206-ABJ Document 108 Filed 09/15/26 Page 49 of 51

50 the UAE was the most “realistic client.” Am. Compl. ¶ 128, and that quotation lifted his words out of context.17
The conspiracy count itself, Count Three, is entirely conclusory; each paragraph makes allegations about the defendants collectively, and not one mentions Vidino specifically. See Am.
Compl. ¶¶ 321–22.

18
While the count incorporates the allegations in Count Two, the substantive RICO count, that puts no meat on the bones as to Vidino, since Count Two does not name him as a defendant or as a member of the alleged “association-in-fact” or “enterprise,” and only one of the many paragraphs and subparagraphs itemizing the alleged predicate acts mentions him: “On January 3, 2018, Badal and Vidino exchanged emails (Brero and Cavin were copied on the exchange) to arrange for Vidino to meet with Badal, Brero, and Cavin in Geneva on January 12, 2018.” Am. Compl. ¶ 311(b).

Therefore, the complaint fails to state the only claim against Vidino, and the case against him will be dismissed.

According to the article, the reporter “said, [Dr. Vidino] must have realized that only the U.A.E. had the means and the motive to pay a private investigator to dig up dirt on Brotherhood-style Islamists across Europe”—to which Dr.
Vidino responded, “[t]hey were the most realistic client” but that “it wasn’t clear cut whether it was the Emiratis, the Saudis, the Israelis, or some private entity in the States.” David D.

That conduct constitutes a conspiracy to violate 18 U.S.C. § 962(c), in violation of 18 U.S.C.

CONCLUSION
For all of the reasons set forth above, defendant UAE’s motion to dismiss for lack of subject matter jurisdiction is GRANTED; the motions to dismiss for lack of personal jurisdiction filed by the Alp defendants – Alp, Diligence, Brero, Badal, and Cavin – and by Besson are GRANTED; and Vidino’s motion to dismiss for failure to state a claim is GRANTED. A separate order will issue.

AMY BERMAN JACKSON
United States District Judge
